quarrel in Part I where councilor W. even says: "Those are
infamous words you are using. " 6 Because of the significance
of the subject one forgets how the antagonists look, forgives
the impassioned man his violent outburst. But Arnold comes
out so clearly that one cannot get him out of mind; there is
nothing to help one forget, the whole thing revolves around
nothing, and Arnold appears as the self-satisfied representa-
tive of emptiness and conceitedness, his irony admired and
prized by the commercial councilor's wife. Take the scene in
the arbor in Part I where Ferdinand W. in the presence of
Claudine and Lusard casually speaks of the relationship be-
tween Madame W. and Dalund; we find it unseemly but for-
get it almost at once simply because there is something to re-
collect. Ferdinand is impassioned, misled and carried away by
modern ideas. Claudine is not inquisitive, but in the throes of
erotic love a disquieting and tempting light dawns on her.
Compare it with the scene in Part 11 where Arnold comes to
invite Mrs. Waller and where her relation to the equerry is
coquettishly mentioned. It has to be that way: the whole situ-
ation must obtrude upon memory. But who gets the credit?
Yes, it is the author, who knows both what needs to be done
and how to do it. As a matter of fact the scene in Part I could
be done by an amateur. There is no dialogue; it is Ferdinand
W. who fervently unburdens himself. In Part 11, however, we
find masterful craftsmanship in reproducing the com-
monplace in the brief rejoinders, the constant interweaving of
situation and dialogue, the authenticity of even the most
casual gesture, the vividness, the colorfulness, even though all
in all it is quite properly kept in the dull shade of triviality.
Take Colette, Arnold, the equerry, all the representatives of
the present age--they come across clearly and unforgettably.


II An Esthetic /nterpretatior1
39
In the presence of deeply felt essential passion, the physical
surroundings are forgotten. But in Part II the author is very
painstaking (simply out of predilection for the task) in ex-
pertly sketching the daily details of everyday life. Here there
is no great rush to the important festival occasion, here one
does not close oneself up idyllically in an abandoned woman's
quiet inwardness; no, here everything is just about equally
important, making the reproduction difficult, somewhat like
painting without light and shadow and still producing an ef-
fect, a work of art. Thus we are not abruptly catapulted into
the reception room where everyone is enjoying spirited talk,
lively conversation, and the fine tone of cultured companion-
ship-no, the author honors his task. Outside the reception
room we hear bickering from inside; we wait outside while
the wife vocalizes; near the stairs the commercial councilor's
wife begins to scold the equerry; the maid joins in the act, as
well as small children and a schoolgirl-precisely because
there is no sense of propriety to establish a distance and separa-
tion, nothing heroic and grand to elevate and distinguish;
there is no stimulating and ennobling event, no idea to mar-
shal the masses in picturesque unanimity or raise the individ-
ual to heroic stature, but everything is movement and postur-
ing.
In short, the author's impartiality in relation to the two
parts perhaps may be expressed this way: if Part I is more cap-
tivating because of its authentic portrayal of great passions,
then Part n is more entertaining, especially the more one reads
it.
So much for the impartiality; another question is: to what
extent does this novel really acknowledge the same conception of ex-
istence which is essential to A Story of Everyday Life? As men-
tioned before, this view is midway between the esthetic and
the religious. Let us consider the existential relations of the
main characters in the novel.
In Part I the denouement in the relation between Claudine
and Lusard is romantically created by constancy; she sticks it out
and thus gets Lusard. And her constancy is mainly a natural
endowment, a matter of immediacy and romantic erotic love,
VIII
37


VIII
JH
40
Two Ages
for the ethical is not decisively contributory, the ethical posi-
tion that Claudine can save her honor only by honoring her-
self and that therefore every new association, every profana-
tion that diminishes inwardness, is a judgment upon her. Her
romanticism is sheer romanticism, not transformed and
sanctified by the ethical. The separation from Lusard places
her in a state of suspension in which she remains romantically
oriented to Lusard, but, still oriented by a romantic desire for
reunion with him, she does not transcend her relationship to
Lusard through an ethical deepening7 of her inner being. In
Part n the denouement in the relationship between Mariane
and Frederick is developed romantically with the help of fate:
Lusard comes forward with financial assistance. The only
question is whether it would not have been more in the spirit
of the older story of everyday life to depict the baron (in Part
1) somewhat differently and then have him be united with
Claudine, to have Mariane suffer the pain of separation and
then provide her with a new consolation. But even if my
comment is acknowledged to be correct, it by no means jus-
tifies an objection, simply because essentially the author is de-
picting the two ages.
Even less can one charge the author with trying to make the
natural superior to the ethical in the relation between Madame
W. and Dalund in Part 1, as ifjaithfulness in an illicit relation
could make this permissible; nor can the author be charged
with presenting the relation as even exercising an ennobling
influence on Madame W. in her marital life with her husband,
for this, after all, is a reproduction of the character of the age.
This is also the case in Part II, where breaking an engagement
out of fear of financial difficulties is made to seem a legitimate
expression of passion. From an ethical point of view, the former
must be regarded as reprehensible and the ennobling as an illu-
sion, for no presumptive right8 can be acquired in any relation
on the basis of what is forbidden; the lapse of time after the
offense is only a repetition of it, and ennoblement by means of
depravity is like the nobility of a man who cannot get along
without money, acquires his wealth unlawfully, and then uses
it to do considerable good. From an esthetic point of view the lat-
ter must be interpreted as comic. As a matter of fact, in Part II the


II An Esthetic Interpretation
41
present age also delivers judgment on the relation between
Madame W. and Dalund, and the author is prevented from
revealing his own views by the nature of the task. Had there
been yet another age, it is not inconceivable that in a rendition
of it Bergland's intended flight from marriage would be in-
terpreted as comic.
Now, attempting to describe the author's superb charac-
terization and allowing myself only a sidelong glance at the
reflexion of the age, I will trace the psychological develop-
ment of the various main characters, showing how and to
what extent their presuppositions control crucial events. The
characteristics of the age will be the chief concern in the next
section. The critic is obliged to assume the double approach
by which the story has made its task so difficult. The author
does not dare to present the age as having automatic conse-
quences in the individuals, for then he would transgress his
task as novelist and merely describe the age and illustrate it by
examples, instead of viewing the reflexion in domestic life and
through it illuminating the age. Action must always occur
through the psychological middle term of the individual. It
would be wrong for an author simply to say: In a revolu-
tionary age a woman is allowed, if she is discreet about it, to
have an illicit relation to a man-as I have portrayed in the
relation between Madame W. and Dalund. The relation be-
tween them must be psychologically motivated, and only
then can there be any mention of the special character of the
age as influencing or permitting this manifestation. In any and
every age it can happen that a young girl falls in love with a
man and after hearing nothing from him in a long time be-
lieves him dead and then marries, whereupon the object of
her first love reappears, doubly dangerous because she is un-
happy. If this is the case, and moreover if the characters are
psychologically portrayed, then the distinctive character of
the age can determine what expression this conflict gets: in
another age, for example, it might have been expressed by the
collapse of the marriage so that she could get her beloved or
by the unhappy lovers' bowing to the sanctity of marriage
and resolving never to see each other again, etc. The author
may not say: The age of revolution was an age of romantic
VIII
.w


VIII
40
42
Two Ages
girls, and this I have portrayed in Claudine. No, the author
must psychologically motivate Claudine's romanticism, and
after that is done the age can determine the particular direc-
tion it takes. In the Middle Ages a romantic girl similarly
motivated would have entered a convent, in a prosaic age she
would have sighed deeply and married, in the period ofLouis
XIV she perhaps would have become a risque countess, etc.
Claudine. Narrowly brought up by Aunt Malfred and thus
in a spiritual-intellectual sense simultaneously stunted and too
accelerated in growth, almost cheated of any impression of
life, she is now transplanted to the Waller household where
she finds the soil and opportunities for development. What
might have ended psychologically either in timid insecurity
or in a desperate breaking out of the narrow-minded aunt's
pinched parlor is now happily transfigured into youth's joy-
ous greenery under the most favorable conditions possible,
even though perhaps dangerous. The freedom she enjoys in
the Waller house, the joy so abundantly offered, the affection,
almost partiality, for the young girl do not spoil her, because
she has learned discretion and discrimination, but refine her in
childlike gratitude "for all the blessings she enjoys in this dear
household. "9 And when the folding doors are opened to a
broad outlook upon a world-historical event, when the
French legation, emissaries from that enchanted theater, show
up in the Waller house and magically touch Claudine's youth-
ful mind with an idea, she is at her highest peak. Here again
the romantic is implicit in the contact that evokes the imag-
ination. If the Wallers had traveled to Paris and taken Clau-
dine along, the effect on her would not have been nearly as
great as the tangential contact that does not overwhelm but
simply inflames the imagination totally. Therefore when
Lusard, unnoticed by all those present and understood only
by Claudine, kneels and kisses the hem ofher dress, Lusard,
who as a foreigner who belongs to little Denmark only
through Claudine and this little symbolic act, this is the au-
thor's ingeniously creative expression for the romantic in
Claudine's whole situation. If Lusard had taken her home
with him to France as his bride, she would not have devel-
oped as she did in this romantic moment of contact, which is


II An Esthetic Interpretation
43
also the moment of parting. A girl can make a double demand
in love if she demands everything: that he be the beloved and
also that he have an idea to give her-then the equation of
erotic love is absolute and complete.
All passion is like sailing: the wind must be sufficiently
forceful to stretch the sail with one uno tenore [continuous J
gust, there must not be too much flapping of the sails and
tacking before reaching deep water, there must not be too
many preliminaries and prior consultations. It is a matter of
passion getting the power and dominion to take complete
control of the unprepared. For instance, Claudine stands all
alone with a world in her soul: then comes the hint to her
from Lusard. This is the romantic element in the author's
superbly planned situations. The power of erotic love is like
the soaring of that winged steed10-it is in a class by itself.
But for everyday use! Then come background and circum-
stances and things to be taken into consideration and relatives
and friends, furnishing the house, people dropping in to offer
their congratulations-and the whole triumphant rash enter-
prise is transformed into a bourgeois expedition: a dray
wagon with all those passengers shouting "hip, hip," and
then erotic love is the patient beast that is supposed to lug all
this away. For Claudine it is different. Destitute of back-
ground and thereby worthy of dedication, she dreams of no
considerations; for her Lusard is the beloved, and through
him she is touched by an idea. From a romantic point of view,
this is the highest covenant of dedication.
This is the design of the beginning, but the surroundings and
circumstances also encourage and psychologically support the
romantic. When two strapping, strong-armed fellows lead
out the rearing, snorting steed that is going to run in the race,
what do they do? They have to let go. Nothing else?-that is
easy enough, after all. Not so, it is an art; they must know
how to let go with passion; if there is the slightest uncertainty
and hesitation on their part, the steed is already delayed
somewhat; in letting go they must know precisely how to
give the steed impetus. In pushing a boat away from the
shore, the object is to bend forward in order to shove harder
and at the same time to throw oneself back to order to gain
VIII
41


44
Two Ages
momentum from the shove. In connection with all passion,
the most the surrounding world can do for one is to let go
with passion. But this is just the way the circumstances are
arranged for Claudine in the Waller home, and the author's
ingenuity may justly insist upon what may be even more rare
than a cheerful giver and a cheerful worker, a cheerful
critic-that is, one who is happy to see what the author has
done. The merchant Waller is immersed in politics. The
rather secretive nature of the quietly meditative Mrs. W. is so
far removed from encumbering Claudine with curiosity and
sympathy that it is more inclined to tempt and encourage the
young girl's secret love. Dalund is daily preoccupied with
studying the psychic expressions on Mrs. W. 's face (as an old
man he continues his study by contemplating her portrait)
and is anything but inclined to smell out other people's love
affairs, he who seems rather to be afraid of the Frenchmen be-
cause of his own relationship. Claudine is solitary and de-
tached, without the sharing that congratulates as, for exam-
ple, one congratulates a person who has found something by
shouting: "Divs," and which frequently does reduce the love
affair to about half. Ferdinand W., the only one who suspects
the love affair, is an inspired troubadour for the Frenchmen
and Lusard.
This is how the love affair progresses; the author has known
how to plot the relationships to give the romantic element
predominance. But something is still needed, something
which the great poet11 claims is part of all that is required
to lure forth erotic love: the possibility of the lovers' meeting
alone. And even if something is denied-"the moon shining
through the beech trees in springtime"-the author is still of
the opinion that September really should be called the love-
month.12 In pushing a boat away from the shore, the object is
to bend forward in order to shove harder and at the same time
to throw oneself back in order to gain momentum from the
shove, and when a secret lover in greater peril than her be-
loved already believes him killed in a duel, the backward
thrust of this thought of death is the most powerful impetus
to love.


II An Esthetic Interpretation
45
The wounded Lusard is brought to the Waller country
house, and the lovers are provided with an opportunity.
Without any preconceived ideas whatsoever, without any
guidance at all other than the eccentricity of erotic love and of
the age, without a single confidant but love, she becomes a
sacrifice for her love. The psychological premises completely
motivate her lapse from virtue, just as they in turn motivate
her constancy, for her mistake and her virtue are essentially
the same romanticism.
Another question is whether the ideas of the age might not
have made Claudine more clear about her step than she later
proved to be. Her later rather naive ignorance of the result of
her relation to Lusard seems in the delineation to be borrowed
from another kind of romanticism, the pastoral-idyllic. In
fact, it would be characteristic of the age to have Claudine re-
alize that her relation to Lusard was what is usually called
marriage, except that the natural relationship was emanci-
pated from ethical obligation, which was regarded as the ob-
solete aspect of marriage. Sentimental romanticism's too in-
nocent ignorance is highly inappropriate in the revolutionary
age and is rather incidental in the context of a portrayal of the
reflexion of the age of revolution. Lusard is more perceptive
about this and therefore in one of his lines calls her "my little
wife. " 13 Claudine, on the other hand, is more a typical girl
led astray by her love than a girl led astray by the influence of
modern ideas. It is true that Ferdinand Waller discusses these
ideas and informs her of the relation between Madame Waller
and Dalund, but there is no indication of what impression this
made on Claudine, what implications it had for her; on the
contrary, she is no different later from what we call a girl
going astray in a romantic love affair.
Lusard is the fiery, smooth, polished Frenchman, excitable
in all moods including sadness, in every respect made for just
such a relationship as this one to Claudine, but his constancy
does not match his impulsiveness, that is, it is possible for an
individual like that to behave as Lusard did, but it would also
be intelligible if he behaved differently. Although it would be
psychologically justifiable to wonder whether Claudine had
VIII
42


VIII
43
VIII
44
46
Two Ages
been untrue to herself, she would have forgiven Lusard even
if he had been unfaithful to her and would still have gone on
loving him.
Very early in her life, Madame Waller had been the object of
Dalund's love, but, misinterpreting his silence, she married
during his absence. Marriage has not improved her; lacking
inner satisfaction, "she pursued only empty pleasures, found
her greatest bliss in attracting attention and being flattered,*
and regarded her appearance as her most important concern"
(p. 37)-until finally she is ennobled by her relation to
Dalund, which Dalund considers rightfully his as indemnifi-
cation for so many years of faithfulness and a love such as his.
But Madame Waller cannot completely master an inner voice
that reminds her that she is treading a forbidden path, a voice
that according to her own declaration becomes louder pre-
cisely because in her betterment she realizes something she
had not realized in her neglected youth.** Meanwhile Da-
lund, exercising a refining and absolute influence expressive
of his masculine and personal superiority and also using the
ideas of the age, fights for the relationship, even to the point
of jealousy (for example, of the Frenchmen).
• From this description one is almost confused about the age in which the
scene takes place.
•• It would be of psychological interest to develop this conflict in detail: she
is ennobled by an illicit relationship and by means of this ennoblement is en-
abled to perceive properly the illicitness of the relationship. Suppose that the
ennoblement had won such a victory of superior insight that she con-
sequently gave up the relationship! Moreover, it is quite obvious that
Madame Waller is a self-contradiction, and it is precisely the author's psycho-
logical discernment that enabled him to accentuate it. We must neither smile
at the self-contradiction nor promptly consider it resolved because it is per-
ceived, still less hastily condemn it. Indeed, when a man tells us that he him-
self perceives the reprehensibility of his action and yet goes on doing it, let
the ethical judge him and the esthetic find him comic. It is different with a
woman, especially in a situation where her strength is perceptible in the very
weakness of her devotion. Through Dalund she can perceive the illicitness in
the relationship, but without him she cannot see it. When she is femininely
secure in the relationship that is so psychologically satisfying, she can per-
ceive that it is illicit, but if the relationship is broken off, her feminine drive
will become erotically desperate and allow her to forget that it was illicit.


II An Esthetic Interpretation
47
Here again we run into a difficulty, for the relationship be-
tween Madame Waller and Dalund certainly does not give
expression to the idea of the age in such a way that it is seen to
follow from or to be borne out by the idea of the age, but in
the relationship Dalund is fighting for the idea of the age and
is consequently seeking to give new corroboration to an al-
ready existing relationship by getting the stamp of approval
upon it from the idea of the age. From a purely personal point
of view, the relationship itself seems to have started and to
continue as the way these two have resolved a conflict in their
lives. The difficulty is that something seems to be lacking
with respect to the reciprocal transparency demanded by the
twofold task of the novel. The definition of the age demands
the middle term of individuality, the definition of individ-
uality demands the middle term of the age, and the task is to
present novelistically in this transparency a reflexion of the
age in domestic life. Or if someone says that the author
merely wants to show how such a relationship was judged in
a revolutionary age, the answer must be that this task is of a
special kind since in fact the judgment consisted simply in ig-
noring it, also that Ferdinand Waller is the only person who
mentions it and we learn nothing whatsoever as to how much
the others know of it, and, finally, that the relationship itself
is presented. The questionable aspect of all this seems to be
that the author in a preface seems to have set himself a third
task-to compare the two ages. 14 But however superbly the
two parts of the novel are arranged for the comparison, the
author probably should not have hinted at such a thing. 15
The remaining characters are less significant and do not
lend themselves to special comment. Nor will I venture to
mention or cite examples of the many excellent details woven
into the unfolding story: we automatically take them for
granted with the author of A Story of Everyday Life. We de-
light in the reading and do not make ourselves important as a
critic by captiously finding fault with a novel such as this as if
it were a beginner's work, in which the problem would be, as
they say, of finding anything at all to appreciate.
But if I were to point to an individual in Part 1 who at least
vm
45


VIII
46
48
Two Ages
to me appears less prepossessing, I would name the baron and
particularly his venture into the demonic. Apart from this, a
question could be raised as to whether he is so untypical that
the reflexion of the age simply is not recognizable. A foolish
baron, half-drunk besides, who in a fit of shrewish ill-temper
gets the notion of killing little Charles, arouses neither horror
nor interest-that is, the reluctant interest that characterizes
the ambiguity of the demonic. He does not arouse horror, be-
cause in the case of a drunken man one's first thought is that
he will sleep it otf, and he does not arouse interest, partly be-
cause he is drunk and partly because his foolishness is any-
thing but masculine. Nor must it appear as if he horrified
Claudine, for such a thing may be vile and repulsive to her,
but horrifying it is not. And to what extent is this strange
episode psychologically motivated? Even if we assume that
the baron, when the possibility of a relationship to Claudine is
wrecked, returns i:o his old nature, which only she had had
the power to control in the obedience oflove, does it necessar-
ily follow that he can behave that way? After all, he was
good-natured originally, had the external marks of good
breeding, and even at such a moment Claudine's influence on
him must have psychological impact. To me this situation
seems unbecoming and essentially inauthentic. 16
Part II. M ariane. Frightened out of youthful illusion by her
stepmother long, long ago, driven into the busy household
tasks of the distressing workaday world, damaged by the
daily insults, she humbly works out the bitter task of being
more or less a housemaid in her parents' house. Her life is not
unacquainted with all the haughty disdain and at the other ex-
treme the foppish liberties to which such a servant-person can
be subjected, and the bitterness is augmented by her being not
only defenseless but excluded-because she is the daughter.
But on the other hand she learns very early to practice the in-
corruptible nature of a quiet inwardness; 17 she is especially
trained to preserve a secret understanding, since. her whole
environment is damaging in its meaninglessness, because
even with her father in her father's house she qua daughter
maintains only a secret understanding. He dares to speak to


II An Esthetic Interpretation
49
her in a fatherly way only in the hall or in passing or when no
one is looking-just as lovers who do not have their parents'
approval contrive to meet, speak to each other in the hall
when the parents in the living room officially oppose any as-
sociation. If Mariane is in love, she is forced to keep it a secret
lest it trigger new mistreatment: Maren the maid is loved,
someone wants to have "that little slattern, " 18 as her step-
mother calls her; this would put her in the position of being
fired by the boss-woman so that she would not be bothered
by all this silly love business.
And Mariane is in love. In order to explain her psychologi-
cally, we will now trace the motives in relationships superbly
designed by the author. From the standpoint of a gossipy in-
terest, the relationships cannot be considered damaging, but
neither are they beneficial; they do not let go with passion, do
not hinder through misunderstood kindness and concern,
either, yet they do exercise a damaging restraining influence.
No doubt it is true that the life offalling and being in love19 is
inwardness, and that inwardness in itself is what it is, and
therefore that inwardness may be the same, for example,
whether it is someone who goes out on the balcony and gazes
at the starry night or is a prisoner for life who steals a glance at
a single star through a crack-the latter's inwardness is
perhaps the greater-but nevertheless falling in love is like
being born, and what is born is greatly influenced by condi-
tions, the environment with its psychological predisposition.
Mariane wanted to be true to herself with the same inwardness
as Claudine, and yet her love affair would forever be essentially
different from Claudine's.
Being in love is the culmination of a person's purely human
existence, which is a double existence, and for that very rea-
son being in love is simultaneously just as much inwardness
as it is a relation directed outwardly to actuality. Happy erotic
love is the equilibrium of the relation. Less inwardness and a
VIII
dominant relation to actuality is a less beautiful love; a domi-
47
nant inwardness and a lesser relation to actuality tends toward
unhappy love.
So it is with Mariane; she knows the misery of actuality


so
Two Ages
too well, and her environment exacerbates the daily harms
and hurts. Instead of being a source of confident, invincible
courage that in matters of love dares to ask anything of ac-
tuality, convinced in its inspired ignorance that actuality is the
world where love has its home, being in love is for her a
source of sadness. The inwardness may be just the same, just
as a retreat may in fact be just as great an achievement as the
most brilliant victory, but the difference is essential. Instead
of perceiving her being in love as a vocation tendered by a
world that wants to indulge it in everything, she is inwardly
accustomed to resigning herself to renunciation: in the split
second she falls in love she secretly realizes that this, too, will
be forced back into her inward being, will be a secret life
others know nothing of, and will simultaneously be im-
proved health and yet, humanly speaking, an infirmity.
The inwardness may be just the same, just as it is an equally
difficult task to die as it is to live. Inwardness is the genuine
pearl; it is formed, as is known, within the mussel through
the absorption of dew. But according to an ancient author it
makes a difference whether it is morning or evening dew:20 so
also it makes a difference whether the genuine individuality of
the love affair is formed by hope or by recollection.
As is the case with all passion, so it is true of erotic love that
the one initiated into it stands free in the consecrated moment
of falling in love, free on the rash summit of illusion, free and
surveying the whole wide world; no silly, sentimental sym-
pathy is allowed there, but also no teasing idlers who keep the
lightly armed one from scaling the dizzy heights and in that
moment prevent the freestanding lover from catching the vi-
sion. If everything is going to turn out well, this protected
moment must not be denied, this moment when all existence
is enchanted and responds to every hint, when nothing, noth-
ing diminishes erotic love's boundless abandonment in illu-
sion, which occurs privatissime [entirely privately] and is quite
different from all the lovers' caressing, since it is the presup-
position that unites the lovers, is the accelerator, infinity's ad-
vance payment on which they will live for many or for a few
weeks. -This Mariane lacks.


II An Esthetic Interpretation
51
The author has depicted this superbly, and it is important
to the relationship between Mariane and Bergland since also
on her side it psychologically motivates fear of financial diffi-
culties as a possible reason for the separation. Mariane cannot
counteract Bergland's discouragement with love's jubilant
rapture; she does not have love's exulting courage to take
feminine charge of the holy expedition of marriage when the
beloved loses self-possession. Psychologically it would not be
devoid of interest to imagine the relationship along these
lines, although it would fall short of the novel's purpose,
which is concerned also with the age. The author understood
this very well. Mariane is unable to put up any resistance; she
herself is troubled in worrisome reflection, and in her lines
there is the sound of a sad familiarity with actuality, a resigna-
tion with regard even to love as the universally valid claim.
But still there is an inwardness, just as permanent and eternal
as the most rapturous love affair, with essentially the same
strength but manifesting itself in an essentially different way.
Even if she is willing to give up Bergland, she still will not
take back her ring because she will not give up the love.
"Neither will I give back yours. Like my love, I have carried
it secretly for three years, I will not give it up in the years left
to me."21 Here she is at her incomparable best, but the love
affair is not a beckoning signal but rather a withdrawal into
inwardness. The deep inwardness of her love is characterized
"by the quiet virtues"22 of a maiden who dedicates herself to
suffering. She had suffered much from the world of actuality,
but this blow seems to be decisive. So also Caesar remained
erect when the conspirators plunged their daggers into him,
but when he saw that Brutus was a party to it, he wrapped his
cloak about him and gave himself up to death with these
words: You, too, my son Brutus!23 Troubled by reflection,
the greatest resistance she can muster against Bergland's pas-
sion in the passionate tension of impatience is the provisional
protestation "that she will wait patiently" (see p. 269), but
even this resistance is allied with reflection.
How accurate and fitting everything is here! The psycho-
logical delineation and the reflexion of the age. In the palmy
VIII
48


VIII
49
52
Two Ages
days of a raucous, jack-of-all-trades superficiality, the good
may live oppressed and clandestine in an ecclesia pressa, 24 as
Mariane is doing. But on the other hand, in a reflected age
even this deep intensity may not remain altogether undis-
turbed by reflection, even in relation to the one thing it has, a
love affair. A spontaneous, enthusiastic lover in Mariane's po-
sition would consider Bergland mad; if it involved her life in
this way she would be utterly unable to understand the pecu-
niary anxieties, but she would also be utterly unable to under-
stand Mariane's resignation. Such a situation could only be
humorous and Bergland essentially a comic figure, since he
represents a rather practical, prosaic point of view.
25Ferdinand Bergland is an eccentric fellow who is carried
away with the bold possibilities life offers and has included a
love affair in the multiplicity of projects and plans and now is
about to run aground on the consequences. A marriage,
which should be a hyphen for him, assumes a formidable
shape, and he becomes afraid-instead of action and an essen-
tial continuity of passion, there is a desperate once-and-for-all
decision. The apparent power that is involved in making
the decision is actually only the expression of weakness, and
therefore the reason he gives is basically incidental. He could
just as well have given any other reason, because the reason is
really the misrelation between possibility and actuality that
grasps at this particular as if it were the reason. His desperate
decision and resultant action are therefore meaningless, and if
things were to go as he wanted them to, he himself would
amount to essentially nothing. To decide not to will to do this
or that is tantamount to not willing ·and actually is deciding to
amount to essentially nothing. If the person deciding not to
will does not at that very moment enter the service of a higher
idea, his decision and action are futile. This is what deter-
mines the outcome oflife's conflicts and decides if the passion
is justified or not. Ferdinand has not understood himself bet-
ter than before by making his decision but has merely made a
headlong retreat into the darkness; all he does is to referre pedem
r retreat]' and in his retrogression there is no progress in
terms of the ideality of his own personality. His worries


II An Esthetic Interpretation
53
about making a living do not in that form constitute a real
view of existence, and the person who takes it upon himself
~~~
to manage another's life, or at least to disturb it, on the scale
Ferdinand did, even though from an ethical point of view his
intervention always turns out to be a blunder, at least must
have understood himself in the context of a more essential
view of existence, an idea to which he can give his life, 26 par-
ticularly ifhe breaks with Mariane.
Here again how accurate and fitting everything is: the psy-
chological delineation and the reflexion of the age. In another
age perhaps Mariane's patience would have gained at least an
interim-until Ferdinand's worries were dispelled. But our
age, despite all its projects and anticipations and pronounce-
ments and presuppositions, is essentially devoid of passion
from the standpoint of inwardness and the romantic. This is
precisely why the variation appears, an unexpected expres-
sion of despair in a desperate action or decision, for this is the
anarchistic law for the extremes in such a life.
Mrs. Waller, wife of the· commercial councilor. If one may
speak of plated people in the same vein as one makes a distinc-
tion between precious and base metals and has something in
between that is called plated, a composite that can fool a per-
son, then Mrs. Waller may very appropriately be so labelled.
She is a plated past-mistress at being anybody and everybody:
one moment charming and the next almost repulsive, lovable
and yet tiresome in her bustling self-importance, smartly
decked out and yet almost looking like a prostitute in her gar-
nished and gilded get-up, demure and flirtatious, cultured and
yet again painfully distressing to the genuinely cultured,
shrewd about life and yet almost inane, currying favor from
beginning to end. She cannot be characterized in any other
way than by this mixed bill, because she has no substance and
therefore her superficiality must be described by enumerating
some of its points. Her ostentatious virtuosity culminates and
turns back into itself in the smug conceit that she is what a
woman of the world ought to be, which accounts for her
proudly and calmly daring to do anything, trusting in her
reputation. And yet in a profound sense her nature is ambiva-


VIII
51
VIII
52
54
Two Ages
lent, for as the apostle James more or less puts it: purity of
heart is to will one thing. 27 Even Mrs. Waller in Part 1 is in a
certain sense less ambivalent.
How admirably and masterfully the author knows how to
control a glittering and delusive weakness such as this, com-
municating at all times the impression of a fictitious-real char-
acter; likewise the ease with which the interrelated situations
are invented, the naturalness with which the thread of con-
tinuity runs throughout the story,* continually illuminating
Mrs. Waller's lack of character in the momentary mirror of
reflection, fleetingly, for in fact there is nothing to dwell
upon; yes, there must be no tarrying, for then Mrs. W. be-
comes someone else. The art lies in the repetition of the psy-
chological conception in the presentation itself, and as philan-
dering is the unstable emptiness, so throughout the whole
novel Mrs. W. is an unstable flurry of busyness, represented
in her transitory relations to the older and younger men, to
her own children, to her husband, to Mariane, to the maid.
The modulation in the relations of the five female characters is
especially superb. The exhibitionist maternalism of the wife
in relation to the two girls who are already trained to be "re-
specters of persons," the parody in Colette's imitation of her
"' To cite but one example: the scene in the commercial councilor's house
when Lusard, weary of listening to Mrs. Waller's bravura music, withdraws
to the study to talk with councilor Dalund. The melancholy, pleasant at-
mosphere surrounding the two nostalgics conversing about the past-until
Arnold, when the music has stopped toward the end of the conversation, un-
suspectingly sits down behind the open door and overhears the conversation,
whereupon Mrs. W. and the equerry enter and the conversation of the pres-
ent age begins. What realism-! do not believe even the severest critic could
find anything to object to here or charge that it is not true to life. What an
ingenious scheme to bring the two ages together this way! In the intimacy of
the study, the past lives and is relived in recollection, and out in the drawing
room, framing the scene, the present age is loud-mouthed, yes, in its indis-
criminating brashness is even on the way to publicizing the secrets of the
study. Just as the best horseman is said to guide his snorting steed with a
thread, giving the appearance that the horse has the upper hand, but as a mat-
ter of fact this illusion merely expresses that the rider is absolutely in control,
so also the author guides the movement of the situation so naturally that there
seems to be no design, whereas the illusion is the very expression of the om-
nipresent design, which manifests itself simply by concealing itself.


II An Esthetic Interpretation
55
mother, which perhaps contributed to the mother's becoming
bored with her,* and finally the profound satire on the wife
implicit in Mariane's quiet nature.
If in describing a person's essential nature one recalls Pliny's
famous words: omnia ad conscientiam, nil ad ostentationem (ev-
erything is measured by a good conscience, not by appear-
ances J, 28 one need only reverse the words (omnia ad ostenta-
tionem, nil ad conscientiam) to describe Mrs. Waller's nature,
which is either an inversion or a perversion. But it should be
pointed out once again, as is proper for a critic in all fairness
to the copiousness of the author, that it takes far greater skill
to fashion such a character than to describe an actual person.
Some may even find my remarks about this character really
significant: yes, it is not inconceivable these days for some-
one to be foolish enough to find my account in its brevity to
be more thoroughgoing than the author's. Alas, this is the
great mistake, one that could happen only in our present age,
which pontificates and thereby does disservice to every work
of art, for it is a far cry from this brevity to the work of art, to
being able to create such a character. Like many other people,
modern critics continually let themselves be fooled by
abstract possibility and the foreshortened perspective, 29
which are anything but creative, by great projects and sum-
marizing surveys, which are anything but productive. No,
whatever one thinks of representing an age by such a feminine
figure, one thing is certain, the character is a masterpiece in
the portrayal of superficiality, masterful all the way to the
most insignificant detail, yes, to the tip of the rose-red tongue
she sticks out at her husband-a swift and secret response to
an unspoken accusation (p. 216).
Councilor Dalund. A venerable old gentleman is in himself a
vm
53
pleasant sight, but Dalund has an added interest for the reader
in that we knew him in his youth. Of course, in one sense any
biography can produce the same effect, but yet it is only in
one sense, for it produces no illusion as does the novel, which
does not make us directly contemporary with Dalund young
* In other words, what is essential and true may be possessed by several
people simultaneously, but affectation is always domineering and envious.


VIII
54
56
Two Ages
and Dalund old but makes us contemporary with him in two
different eras.
But is he kept psychologically consistent? Without a doubt.
But what is the origin of the satirical trait, which really was
not noticeable in his youth and which the author did not
allow to emerge? In Part 1 he is essentially the young com-
monsensical man with the intelligent face, the talented lawyer
who will not let himself be carried away by factions; even
though a world-historical event is the divisive force that mo-
mentously alienates men in heated party feeling. He keeps his
equilibrium mainly through his intelligence and is hot-headed
only in his relation to Mrs. Waller. Sagacity, neutrality, and
legal acuteness are essential to a satirist, but it does not follow
that whenever these elements are present there is a satirist.
But times have changed and have also changed him some-
what, and in and through this change it is psychologically
consistent for him to become satirical. On the whole, neu-
trality is conceivable only when the factors are essentially sig-
nificant, when the two are indeed separated by an idea. How-
ever, if the times are trivial, if the factional disagreement is a
kind of dramatic pretense of actually playing at revolution,
then the person who in catastrophic times was neutral will be-
come a satirist. Neutrality is founded on a positive acknowl-
edgment of the respective validity, val ore intrinseco [according
to the intrinsic value], of each faction. Remove the presuppo-
sition of this condition for neutrality and eo ipso the neutrality
is negatively qualified as satire, because to be neutral in rela-
tion to a chimerical either/or means either that one is a fool,
since the neutrality is just as foolish as the trifles by which it
defines itself negatively, something only a nobody can do
successfully, or that one, by being essentially somebody, is eo
ipso satirical. Thus the man is essentially the same: the change
is merely a reflexion of the change of the age.
Dalund has also mellowed.* This, too, is quite as it should
be, not least because it is a compensation to the gifted man in
his old age, whereas only the more mediocre person gets
* This too shows that the satirical in him is not to be confused with any
bitterness of old age.


II An Esthetic Interpretation
57
peevish as an oldster. But is this also a reflexion of the change
of the age? He feels unique, he, an old man in his decline, but
he has been part of a more significant era, an important past.
It is precisely this which mellows him, gives him backbone,
and makes him gentle through recollection's secret communi-
cation with the past. Being an old man, he belongs to two
ages; thus he is bifrontal: gentle when he looks back and a bit
satirical when he looks at the present age-and generally a
lovable old man. He is not dull and apathetic, perhaps also be-
cause he still has something to fight for, because the love affair
still lives in his soul. He still battles the enemy-the suspense
of illicit possession; he still battles against doubt: whether
faithfulness does indeed atone for guilt; he is still troubled
about whether faithfulness will indeed be able to reunite him
with his heart's desire, for just as Madame Waller's lawful
husband once stood in the way, now eternity seems to be
doing that.
Charles Lusard. The novel presents two ages. The two parts
are separate from each other, but there is still a connecting
link, an ingenious kind of transition that unites them. This
figure is Charles Lusard. He is too young to belong to the
past, and he is not wholly unaffected by the reflectiveness of
the present age. He is a stable personality, and here again we
see a union of a psychological point of view and a reflexion of
the age. His childhood impressions and later the wonderful
ceremoniousness of family life in his parental home must nat-
urally awaken a certain sad reverie in his soul, which quite
consistently discharged its youthful cravings in foreign travels
and in an imaginary striving which admittedly did not bring
him into any personal relationships precisely because his
melancholy had an isolating effect. That is how a romantic in-
closing reserve cleaves a young man's soul. Youth's desire for
comradeship is not felt because he already is living in recollec-
tion. Not even erotic love has its season, for in inwardness he
is already betrothed, but the solitary's youthful external en-
deavors are directed to extraneous enterprises which can
occupy him without his getting involved essentially with
anyone, without missing anyone precisely because in his re-
VIII
55


VIII
56
58
Two Ages
collecting he does miss someone. He traveled both in the old
world and the new and then returned to his estate. Con-
sequently he is an individuality who has remained constant,
but this also demands an element of reflection and of self-
reflection, and on the other hand this again is a reftexion of the
age which itselflacks decisiveness and has not had enough in-
fluence on him to sweep him along. Therefore he essentially is
not a part of either age. The idea he represents-after having
renounced his own life in order to make a few people
happy-is essentially alien to both ages. Precisely by being
constant Charles Lusard is the transition. In a revolutionary
age such an individual no doubt would become a party-liner;
in the present age he perhaps would not have achieved the
harmonious and thoroughly cultured equilibrium that charac-
terizes his noble and independent bearing, even though his
soul somewhat pensively trails after a memory. By being an
unhappy individual (and so we must call someone who is
unmotivated by any contemporary age or any relationship),
he is happy but still is not a happy individual.
The amazing thing about the novel is that everything is so
categorically true, 30 although when one reads it the work
seems so unpretentious that the superficial reader-ifhe hap-
pens to see this review-perhaps will be startled and will
think I am making more of it than it is. Yet I cannot accept
such a compliment from the reader but must convey it to the
proper person, the author, who, even ifl did understand all of
it, is, after all, first the creator and is also the one who had the
art to conceal that fact and, finally, perhaps knows and puts
into the novel much that I have not been able to discover.
There is yet another character in the second part of the
novel: Johannes Milner, the overseer of the estate, a jovial fel-
low who, after a desperate career crisis without livelihood and
suspicion of being an agitator, found a job with Lusard that
made him a happy man. In the owner's absence, he has wisely
managed the estate and now in his unqualified joy over life
occasionally entertains the wish to see Lusard settled in mar-
riage. It is clear that Johannes Milner has been a very active
man on the estate, but in the novel he seems to be rather un-


II An Esthetic Interpretation
59
occupied. Yet a friendship such as the one existing between
Milner and Lusard is not without significance in explaining
Lusard. In fact, a friendly relationship points up Lusard as a
lonely figure. It is not the unanimity of two like-minded peo-
ple, not a fond urge for familiarity. Milner, comfortably set-
tled personally and maritally and satisfied with life, neverthe-
less finds something dead and ruined, something sad, 31 in
Lusard's long trips abroad "all by himself." Add to that the
fact that Lusard is Milner's benefactor, and however careful
the noble, cultured man is about toning down any echo of
disparity in the relationship, it is still there, reinforcing the
superiority of the solitary one and increasing the distance.
Because all the details in Part II are weighted with signifi-
cance, it could r..ot occur to me to cite particular ones. Only
one single line32 by Lusard grated on me at least, because I did
not think it consistent of Lusard to say this to a lady. The
statement may be altogether true, but the dubious aspect is its
relation to the speaker and speaker's situation.


VIII
57
III
Conclusions from a Consideration
of the Two Ages 1
I now have come to the final section and face the problem the
author has posed for a critic by his own suggestion of supply-
ing a criticism. I wish the author had not done it. I certainly
do not say that on my own account, for in another sense it
would be to my advantage to accept the clues offered, but
nevertheless I will not copy them and pass them off as my
own criticisms. But in my opinion the book has been harmed
by this preface, 2 precisely because it can prompt rash and im-
pulsive people and loose tongues to say: "Is that all? After all,
the whole thing can be said on one page." It is certainly true
that what a gossip or a sassy, naughty child says makes no
difference whatsoever, but when it concerns a book by a repu-
table, distinguished, and established literary figure, some-
thing else is manifestly more desirable, something which we,
who perhaps are too accustomed to malice, almost forget,
that the gossip or the naughty child should simply have kept
quiet. Something that may be insignificant if it happens to
others proves to be all out of proportion when it happens to a
person of importance, and what is tolerable when it happens
to oneself distresses one when it happens to a man of distinc-
tion. When a person is wearing rubbers because of the muddy
roads, he cheerfully takes his chances, but when the same per-
son sees a young girl, for example, who has solved the prob-
lem by walking a rather long way from her home without
getting muddy at all and blissfully conscious of this trips mer-
rily along-and then along comes a boor who in passing
splashes her with mud, the person wearing rubbers is dis-
tressed. It seems to him to be an injustice on the part oflife to


Ill Conclusions: The Age of Revolution
61
pile up incongruities simultaneously; it depresses him that the
street is for everybody instead of everybody's going out of his
way for the young girl-and in Danish literature at present
there is no right of way and no police whatsoever w,hq at least
could keep the street-corner loafers and porters and louts off
the sidewalk.
THE AGE OF REVOLUTION3
The issue here is not an ethical-philosophical evaluation of its
justification but the consequences in which its concept is re-
flected, and the task is to suggest them only in a general way,
corresponding to the details of the author's fictional presenta-
tion.
The age of revolution is essentially passionate, and there-
fore it essentially has form. Even the most vehement expres-
sion of an essential passion4 eo ipso has form, 5 for this is the
expression itself, and therefore also has in its form an apol-
ogy, an element of reconciliation. Only for a completely ex-
ternal and indifferent dialectic is the form not the alter ego of
content and thereby the content itself, but rather an irrelevant
third something. For example, every letter that bears the
mark of inwardness in the expression of an essential felt pas-
sion has eo ipso form. But whether the letter is folded form-
lessly could occupy only a totally external dialectic with the
apparent importance ofbeing a question of form.
The age of revolution is essentially passionate and therefore
essentially has culture. In other words, the tension and resil-
ience of the inner being are the measure of essential culture.*
A maidservant genuinely in love is essentially cultured; a
peasant with his mind passionately and powerfully made up is
* Being highminded and humane, the author of A Story if Everyday Life
has frequently been attentive to this and in this novel, too, has sketched with
a few strokes such a character in Mrs. Lyng, the skipper's widow. "She had
experienced a great deal in life and was one of those people never adequately
appreciated, in whom kindness and gentleness take the place of upbringing
and culture, whom recollection of the sufferings and irYustices they have
undergone teaches refinement regardless of their external station in life" (p.
87).
VIII
SH
VIII
5H
VIII
SH


VIII
59
62
Two Ages
essentially cultured. Whereas there is only affectation, the pre-
tense of form, in the external piecemeal training correlative
with an interior emptiness, the flamboyant colors of swagger-
ing weeds in contrast to the humble bowing of the blessed
grain, the mechanical counting of the beat correlative with the
lacklustre of the dance, the painstaking decoration of the
bookbinding correlative with the deficiency of the book. -If
the novel stresses the cultured in the first age, it does it also by
an accidental addition, inasmuch as the age of revolution in
Copenhagen is represented by an exceptional little band of
exceptionally cultured Frenchmen.
The age of revolution is essentially passionate; therefore it
must be able to be violent, riotous, wild, ruthless toward ev-
erything but its idea, but precisely because it still has one
motivation, it is less open to the charge of crudeness. However
externally oriented his ambitions, the person who is essen-
tially turned inward because he is essentially impassioned for
an idea is never crude. A hurricane, an earthquake, the raging
of the elements are not exactly what one calls damp, raw air;
to define that, one might very well say: It is an uncomfortable
lack of specific quality. So also in the world of individuals. If
the essential passion is taken away, the one motivation, and
everything becomes meaningless externality, devoid of char-
acter, then the spring of ideality stops flowing and life to-
gether becomes stagnant water-this is crudeness. Purely
dialectically the relations are as follows, and let us think them
through dialectically without considering any specific age.
When individuals (each one individually) are essentially and
passionately related to an idea6 and together are essentially re-
lated to the same idea, the relation is optimal and normative. 7
Individually the relation separates them (each one has himself
for himself), and ideally it unites them. Where there is essen-
tial inwardness, there is a decent modesty between man and
man that prevents crude aggressiveness; in the relation of
unanimity to the idea there is the elevation that again in con-
sideration of the whole forgets the accidentality of details.
Thus the individuals never come too close to each other in the
herd sense, 8 simply because they are united on the basis of an


III Conclusions: The Age of Revolution
63
ideal distance. The unanimity of separation is indeed fully or-
chestrated music. On the other hand, if individuals relate to
an idea merely en masse (consequently without the individual
separation of inwardness), we get violence, anarchy, riotous-
ness; but if there is no idea for the individuals en masse and no
individually separating essential inwardness, either, then we
have crudeness. The harmony of the spheres is the unity of
each planet relating to itself and to the whole. Take away the
relations, and there will be chaos. But in the world of indi-
viduals the relation is not the only constituting factor and
therefore there are two forms. Remove the relation to one-
self, and we have the tumultuous self-relating of the mass to
an idea; but remove this as well, and we have crudeness. Then
people shove and press and rub against each other in pointless
externality, for there is no deep inward decency that decor-
ously distances the one from the other; thus there is turmoil
and commotion that ends in nothing. No one has anything
for himself, and united they possess nothing, either: so they
become troublesome and wrangle. Then it is not even the gay
and lively songs of conviviality that unite friends; then it is
not the dithyrambic songs of revolt that collect the crowds;
then it is not the sublime rhythms of religious fervor that
under divine supervision muster the countless generations to
review before the heavenly hosts. No, then gossip and rumor
and specious importance and apathetic envy become a surro-
gate for each and all. Individuals do not in inwardness turn
away from each other, do not turn outward in unanimity for
an idea, but mutually turn to each other in a frustrating and
suspicious, aggressive, leveling reciprocity. The avenue of the
idea is blocked off; individuals mutually thwart and con-
travene each other; then selfish and mutual reflexive opposi-
tion is like a swamp--and now they are sitting in it. Instead of
joy there is a kind of sniveling discontent, instead of sorrow a
kind of sullen, dogged tenaciousness, instead of enthusiasm
the garrulous common sense of experience. But it is one thing
to save one's life by the enchantment of story-telling as in A
Thousand and One Nights; it is something else again to shut
oneself out from the ench~ntment of enthusiasm over an idea
VIII
60


VIII
hl
64
Two Ages
and the rebirth of passion-by talkativeness. Suppose that
such an age has invented the swiftest means of transportation
and communication, has unlimited combined financial re-
sources: how ironic that the velocity of the transportation sys-
tem and the speed of communication stand in an inverse rela-
tionship to the dilatoriness of irresolution. The shrewd
superiority that boasts of not letting itself be carried away
(which generally may be all right if one is sitting in a swamp)
is a rather plebian invention.
The age of revolution is essentially passionate and therefore
also has a concept of propriety. It may well be that it has a false
concept of propriety, but it does not lack the concept. It
might be supposed that propriety is a category of the under-
standing, yet it is anything but that. The distinction is some-
thing akin to the difference between prose and poetry. One
might suppose that the lyric would be really free and easy,
and yet it is precisely the lyric that is disciplined and exacting
language, and the stanza itself is not a creation of a narrowing
understanding but on the contrary is the lyric's own happy
invention. So also with propriety. It is feeling's and passion's
own invention, and just as prose is unconstrained language,
so also prosiness is unconstraint that does not know propri-
ety; it is not the lack of discipline that produces terrible prosi-
ness but instead a dismal lack of character. Fundamentally,
essential passion is its own guarantee that there is something
sacred, and this gives rise to the determinant propriety. When
passion is essentially present in the pagan, even his idolatry is
not devoid of dev·outness; although he has a false concept, he
has the idea that one should fear God. 9 But prosiness lacks the
concept. Thus when a revolutionary age tolerates a relation to
a married woman, it has an idea of propriety despite its false
concept. The permissibility of the relation, reflected in its im-
permissibility, is therefore expressed by the requirement of
propriety that it be concealed. This concealment in turn indi-
cates that the relation, essentially one of passion under the seal
of silence, gratifies the two. Take away the passion and the
propriety also disappears: then presumably the relation will
not develop, but there is talk about it. Thus even if the age of


III Conclusions: The Age of Revolution
65
revolution wanted to abolish marriage, it did not abolish fall-
ing in love, and for the very reason that where there is an es-
sential passion there is also a propriety.
The age of revolution is essentially passionate andl:therefore
has immediacy. Its immediacy, however, is not the first im-
mediacy, and in the highest sense it is not the final immedi-
acy, 10 either; it is an immediacy of reaction and to that extent is
provisional. This is crucial with respect to the constancy of this
passion. In life it may well be that multiplicity remains true to
itself until the end, but seen in the context of the idea it must
end with the single individual's becoming untrue to himself,
because it is a provisional idea. From the standpoint of the
idea, a person finds definitive rest only in the highest idea,
which is the religious, 11 but it may well be that many remain
true to themselves in the provisional* all their lives. The im-
mediacy of the age of revolution is a restoring of natural rela-
tionships in contrast to a fossilized formalism which, by hav-
ing lost the originality of the ethical, has become a dessicated
ruin, a narrow-hearted custom and practice. Simply as reac-
tion it can be transformed by one single deviation into un-
truth, which in an accidental way accentuates the polemical,
as would be the case if the point were reached that erotic love
is erotic love only when it is adultery.
In the novel the reactionary immediacy of the age of revo-
lution is captured in the reflexion in domestic life. It is re-
flected in the relation between Mrs. Waller and Dalund, in the
relation between father and son (councillor Waller and Fer-
dinand Waller); it is reflected in the political views voiced in
conversations. In the relation between father and son, reac-
tionary immediacy wants to do away with the inexplicables
of piety and make nature the only determinant: thus depend-
ency should be abolished on coming to maturity and parents
and children should be equals and friends. In fact this idea,
yes, even the idea of freedom and equality, is not without
* Lusard remains true to himself in the idea, but yet with the modification
that he is excited about Napoleon, will even tight for him. Ferdinand Waller
is personally changed and confesses to Claudine that basically she was the one
who remained true to the idea.
VIII
62
VIII
hi


VIII
6]
66
Two Ages
form as long as the idea itself is the essential truth inspiring
the enthusiast, for then the inwardness is not abolished. When
the religious idea inspires the Moravian Brethren to express
equality by being brothers and sisters, this is not formlessness
at all, for uniformity is the essential form; nor is it an empty
abstraction as long as inwardness is implicit. Formlessness is
simply absence of content. For example, if a woman in a fit of
boredom got the notion of dolling herself up and dressing in
all her finery, this would be formlessness for the simple rea-
son that there would be no idea behind it. The naked Ar-
chimedes12 is adorned with joy over his discovery and is
therefore essentially dressed. This must be borne in mind es-
pecially in our day when, for example, a formal skill is in the
process of becoming formless simply because nothing has es-
sential meaning, everything has so little meaning.
The age of revolution is essentially passionate; therefore it
is essentially revelation, revelation by a manifestation of en-
ergy that unquestionably is a definite something and does not
deceptively change under the influence of conjectural criti-
cism concerning what the age really wants.
The age of revolution is essentially passionate; therefore it
has not nullified the principle if contradiction 13 and can become
either good or evil, and whichever way is chosen, the impetus
of passion is such that the trace of an action marking its prog-
ress or its taking a wrong direction must be perceptible. It is
obliged to make a decision, but this again is the saving factor,
for decision is the little magic word that existence respects. If,
however, the individual refuses to act, existence cannot help.
To be like that King Agrippa, 14 not far from believing or act-
ing, is the most enervating state imaginable if one remains in
it very long. Claudine goes astray. Her decision is stamped by
the impetus of passion, but this again supports her. Her
TTATJpocf>opux EL'O" rrcxifo~ [fullness of pathos] makes her fall, but
it also strengthens her again. Thus an age that is very reflec-
tive cannot for that reason be summarily accused of being
powerless, for it perhaps has great power, but it goes to waste
in the fruitlessness of reflection. It does not take nearly as
much effort to achieve something with the support of an illu-


Ill Cotu1usions: The Age ~f Revolution
67
sion 15 as it does when all illusions are lost. And just as scurvy
is cured by green vegetables, so a person worn out in reflection
perhaps does not need strength as much as a little illusion.
It is .not uncommon to hear a man who has become con-
fused about what he should do in a particular situation com-
plain about the unique nature of the situation, thinking that he
could easily act if the situation were a great event with only
one either/or. This is a mistake and a hallucination of the un-
derstanding. There is no such situation. The presence of the
crucial either/or depends upon the individual's own impas-
sioned desire directed toward acting decisively, upon the in-
dividual's own intrinsic competence, and therefore a compe-
tent man covets an either/or in every situation because he does
not want anything more. But as soon as the individual no
longer has essential enthusiasm in his passion but is spoiled by
letting his understanding frustrate him every time he is
going to act, he never in his life discovers the disjunction.
And even if his penetrating, resourceful understanding is
adequate for managing an entire household, he still has not
~~~
had an understanding of his life in advance or in the moment
of action, and it cannot be understood afterwards, either, be-
cause the action essentially did not take place, and the coher-
ence of his life became a garrulous continuation or a con-
tinued garrulity, a participial or infinitive phrase in which the
subject must be understood or, more correctly, cannot be lo-
cated at all because, as the grammarians say, the meaning does
not make it clear for the simple reason that it lacks meaning.
The whole thing becomes a flux, a blend of a little resolution
and a little situation, a little prudence and a little courage, a
little probability and a little faith, a little action and a little in-
cident. Anyone who has made the fraudulent trade of getting
abnormally good sense by losing the capacity to will and the
passion to act is very inclined to stiffen his spinelessness with
various and sundry predeliberations that feel their way ahead
and various and sundry postmortem reinterpretations of what
happened. Compared to this, an action is a brief something
and apparently a poor something, yet it is in fact a definite
something. The other is more splendid, but for all that it is a


VIII
64
VIII
65
68
Two Ages
splendid shabbiness. A Roman emperor sitting at the table
surrounded by his bodyguard is a magnificent sight, but
when the reason is fear, the magnificence pales. So also when
the individual does not dare stand taciturnly by his word,
does not stand freely and confidently on the pedestal of a con-
scious act, but is surrounded by a host of deliberations before
and after that render him incapable of getting his eye on the
action. Just as an oldster supportively puts his hand on the
small of his back and leans on a cane, so also abnormal com-
mon sense uses reflection in advance as a crutch and comes to
its own aid afterwards with reinterpretive reflection-and
why? Simply because it did not eventuate in action. Instead of
bringing forth the child of a god, the silent, taciturn decision,
the generation gives birth to a changeling of the understand-
ing that has things at its fingertips.
THE PRESENT AGE
Again the task here, as I see it, in critical service to the novel,
is to advance in a more general observation the specific ele-
ments that the author has depicted with literary skill.
16The present age is essentially a sensible, rejlecting 11 age, de-
void of passion, flaring up in superficial, short-lived enthusiasm and
prudentially relaxing in indolence.
If we had statistics on the use of prudence from generation
to generation as we have them on the consumption of liquor,
we would be amazed to see the enormous quantity used these
days, what a quantity of weighing and deliberating and con-
sidering even a small non-official family uses although it has
ample income, what a quantity even children and young peo-
ple use, for just as the children's crusade symbolizes the Mid-
dle Ages, so the shrewdness of children symbolizes ours. I
wonder if there is a person anymore who ever makes just one
big stupid blunder. Not even a suicide these days does away
with himselfin desperation but deliberates on this step so long
and so sensibly that he is strangled by calculation, making it a
moot point whether or not he can really be called a suicide,
inasmuch as it was in fact the deliberating that took his life. A
premeditated suicide he was not, but rather a suicide by means


III Conclusions: The Present Age
69
of premeditation. Therefore it would be extremely difficult to
be a prosecuting attorney against an age such as this, because
the whole generation is expert on legal matters, and its compe-
tence, its sensibleness, its technical skill consist in letting mat-
ters reach a verdict and decision without ever acting.
If we say of a revolutionary age that it goes astray, then we
must say of the present age that it is going badly. The individ-
ual and the generation are continually contradicting them-
selves and each other, and therefore it would be impossible
for a prosecuting attorney to establish any fact, because there
is none. From the abundance of circumstantial evidence, one
might conclude either that something extraordinary had hap-
pened or is about to happen. But that would be a wrong con-
clusion, for circumstantial evidence is the present age's only
attempt at a show of strength, and its inventiveness and tech-
nical skill in contriving spellbinding mirages and the rashness
of its flares of enthusiasm employing the misleading shortcuts
of proposed formal changes rate just as high in calculating
shrewdness and negative use of power as does the energetic
and creative passion in the performance of the age of revolu-
tion. Exhausted by its chimerical exertions, the present age
then relaxes temporarily in complete indolence. Its condition
is like that of the stay-abed in the morning who has big
dreams, then torpor, followed by a witty or ingenious inspi-
ration to excuse staying in bed.
The single individual (however well-intentioned many of
them are, however much energy they might have if they
could ever come to use it) has not fomented enough passion in
himself to tear himself out of the web of reflection and the
seductive ambiguity of reflection. The environment, the con-
temporary age, has neither events nor integrated passion but
in a negative unity creates a reflective opposition that toys for
a moment with the unreal prospect and then resorts to the
brilliant equivocation that the smartest thing has been done,
after all, by doing nothing. Vis inertia? [the force of inertia] is
at the bottom of the age's tergiversation, 18 and every passive
do-nothing congratulates himself on being the original
inventor-and becomes even more clever. Just as weapons
VIII
66


VIII
67
70
Two Ages
were freely distributed in the age of revolution and the insig-
nia of the enterprise was conferred publicly during the cru-
sades, 19 so today we are everywhere lavishly regaled with
pragmatic rules, a calculus of considerations, etc. If a whole
generation could be presumed to have the diplomatic task of
procrastinating and of continually frustrating any action and
yet make it seem as if something were happening, then we
cannot deny that our age is performing just as amazingly as
the age of revolution. If someone were to make an experi-
ment and forget all he knows about the age and the plain fact
of its habitual and excessive relativity, if he were to come as if
from another world and were to read some books, an article
in the paper, or merely talk with a passer-by, he would get
the impression: By Jove, something is going to happen this
very night-or something must have happened the night be-
fore last! 20
In contrast to the age of revolution, which took action, the
present age is an age of publicity, the age of miscellaneous an-
nouncements: nothing happens but still there is instant public-
ity. An insurrection in this day and age is utterly unimagi-
nable; such a manifestation of power would seem ridiculous
to the calculating sensibleness of the age. However, a political
virtuoso might be able to perform an amazing tour de force of
quite another kind. He would issue invitations to a general
meeting for the purpose of deciding on a revolution, wording
the invitation so cautiously that even the censor would have
to let it pass. On the evening of the meeting, he would so
skillfully create the illusion that they had made a revolution
that everyone would go home quietly, having passed a very
pleasant evening. Acquiring a profound and capacious learn-
ing would be practically unthinkable for young people today;
they would consider it ludicrous. A scientific virtuoso, how-
ever, would be able to negotiate a radically different tour de
force. He would casually outline a few features of a com-
prehensive system and do it in such a way that the reader (of
the prospectus) would get the impression that he had already
read the system. 21 The age of the encyclopedists, the men
who indefatigably wrote folios, is over; now it is the turn of


Ill Conclusions: The Present Age
71
the lightly equipped encyclopedists who dispose of the whole
of existence and all the sciences en passant. A penetrating reli-
gious renunciation of the world and what is of the world, 12
adhered to in daily self-denial, would be inconceivable to the
youth of our day; every second theological graduate, how-
ever, has enough virtuosity to do something far more mar-
velous. He is able to found a social institution with no less a
goal than to save all who are lost.
The age of great and good actions is past; the present age is
the age of anticipation. No one is willing to be satisfied with
doing something specific; everyone wants to luxuriate in the
daydream that he at least may discover a new part of the
world. Our~ is an age of anticipation; even appreciative
acknowledgment is accepted in advance. Just like a young
man who, having resolved to study earnestly for his exams
after September 1, fortifies himself for it by taking a vacation
in the month of August, so the present generation-and this is
much more difficult to understand-seems to have deter-
mined in earnest that the next generation must attend to the
work in earnest, and in order not to frustrate or deter them in
any way, the present generation attends banquets. But there is
a difference: the young man understands that his enterprises
are rash and reckless; the present age is sober and serious-
even at banquets.
Action and decision are just as scarce these days as is the
fun of swimming dangerously for those who swim in shallow
water.Just as an adult, himself reveling in the tossing waves,
calls to those younger: "Come on out, just jump in
quickly"-just so does decision lie in existence, so to speak
(although, of course, it is in the individual), and shouts to the
youth who is not yet enervated by too much reflection and
overwhelmed by the delusions of reflection: "Come on out,
jump in boldly~" Even if it is a rash leap, 23 if only it is deci-
sive, and if you have the makings of a man, the danger and
life's severe judgment upon your recklessness will help you to
become one.
If the treasure every one covets lies far out on a very thin
crust of ice, guarded by the great danger to anyone venturing
VIII
6H


72
Two Ages
so far out, whereas (let us assume this oddity which after all is
odd only in the illustration) closer to shore the ice is thick and
solid-in a passionate age the crowd would loudly cheer the
bold, brave person who skates out on the thin ice. They
would shudder for him and with him in his perilous decision,
would grieve for him ifhe meets his death, and would idealize
him if he gets the treasure. The situation would be entirely
different in a reflective age devoid of passion. In mutual rec-
ognition of shared prudence, they would sensibly agree that it
certainly would not be worth the trouble to skate out on such
thin ice- in fact, it would be foolish and ridiculous. Then an
inspired venture would be transformed into an acrobatic stunt-
in order to do something, for "something has to be done."
They would go out and from their safe vantage point appraise
with the air of connoisseurs the expert skater who can skate
almost to the very edge (that is, as far out as the ice is still safe
and just short ofbeing dangerous) and then tum back. One of
the skaters would be exceptionally skilled, and he would even
be able to perform the stunt of making one seemingly hazard-
ous swoop right at the very edge, causing the spectators to
shout: "Ye gods, he is crazy, he is risking his life." But you
see, he is so exceptionally skillful that he can make a sharp
turn precisely at the extreme edge- that is, where the ice is
still completely safe and still short of being dangerous. Just as
at the theater, the crowd would shout "Bravo!" and acclaim
him and then carry their heroic performer home on their
shoulders and honor him with a sumptuous banquet. Com-
mon sense would become so predominant that it would
change the task itself into an unreal stunt and actuality into a
theater. At the banquet in the evening, the admiration would
resound. But whereas what usually happens where admira-
tion is authentic is that the admirer is inspired by the thought
of being a manjust like the distinguished person, is humbled
by the awareness of not having been able to accomplish this
